- `e2e/specs/chat.spec.ts` — a new chat: the lead agent's team card, confirming it and the reply; teams used without asking; a team of every CLI answering in order (the seven with a sign-in, and the five open agents; a half with more CLIs than the lead agent puts in a new team, five, answers as teams of about the same size, each in a chat of its own, so the seven with a sign-in as teams of four and three); a follow-up question with a team of its own.
- `e2e/specs/activity.spec.ts` — each CLI's thinking, tool call (its own listing tool, with its output) and to-do list (none for Cline and Pi); a reply in Markdown (heading, list, table, code).
- `e2e/specs/approvals.spec.ts` — each CLI asking before a command (Pi and Copilot in edit mode, about `npm publish`): allowed (it runs), declined with a note (shown on the card; passed on to Claude Code, Grok, OpenCode, Goose and Pi, dropped by Codex, Cursor, Muse Code, Cline and OpenHands, and Antigravity and Copilot ending their turn); "Always allow" answering the next request by itself; stopping while a request waits; each CLI with full access running and writing anywhere without asking; a chat that refuses automatically.
- `e2e/specs/files.spec.ts` — edit mode: a file written, `git init`, the changes card and the turn's diff; each CLI in a read-only chat writing nothing (Claude Code and Pi without their file tools, Cursor refused by its own rules, the others asking and declined), and Grok's reason when refused automatically; each CLI's turn recording its own change, in two teams; parallel mode's worktrees and applying one result; the file browser and a file's preview; a folder's ZIP download (its files, no `.git`) and, for one too large, the offer without what git ignores.
- `e2e/specs/folders.spec.ts` — in edit mode, a folder the chat was not given staying out of each CLI's reach when refused automatically (the open agents' writes refused by Harness Relay's own sandbox, Copilot's by its own without asking), and Claude Code, Codex, Cursor and Antigravity writing there once the user allows it; several working directories: folders added and removed in the box, each CLI writing in each folder with the changes card listing them by folder (Grok on Windows told them by its briefing alone, Cursor asking before the second), the file browser's folders, parallel mode's worktree of each folder applied together, and Settings' default working directories. On Linux, the folder dialog (the fake zenity): the folders chosen in it added to the box and the chat starting in them, the dialog opened inside the last folder with several allowed, and in Chinese on a server without a UTF-8 locale its Chinese title and `LC_CTYPE=C.UTF-8`, with Cancel adding nothing.
- `e2e/specs/turns.spec.ts` — asking the user and answering, skipping a question, relay hand-offs, an @mention answered by that member only, passing in a later round, each CLI's reply stopped, its error (an exit, and one inside its protocol) and Retry, its next turn resuming its own session, a member whose CLI lost its session starting again with the chat so far, a message sent mid-reply reaching the next member.
- `e2e/specs/history.spec.ts` — a message edited and sent again (what came after it replaced), Retry on a finished reply, a conversation branched from a reply.
- `e2e/specs/team.spec.ts` — the lead agent adding a member when asked in Team settings, and the new member answering once saved; a template standing in when the lead agent's answer cannot be read; a team proposed for a new question declined, and the current team answering; the add card as tall as the members beside it (its list a named group that scrolls, with a shadow while more are below), listing every CLI on a row of its own, and one tall card stretching only its row.
- `e2e/specs/inputs.spec.ts` — what reaches each CLI besides the prompt: an MCP server from the tool library (its secret kept off the command line), one over HTTP (its secret header too; not Antigravity, whose adapter gives it none), and an attached image (not Cline, which takes none).
- `e2e/specs/sandbox.spec.ts` — Harness Relay's own sandbox around the open agents: in a read-only chat a write the user allows still refused, and a host the run may not reach asked about and refused when declined; an open agent below full access where it runs only with full access (Windows; OpenCode on Linux) not started, saying why; OpenHands on macOS without its terminal switch; Antigravity asking about a project's own setup before agy starts, and not starting when refused; Copilot asking about a project's hooks before it starts, not starting when declined, and once allowed running the turn.
- `e2e/specs/sidebar.spec.ts` — a group and the lead agent sorting a new conversation into it, renaming and deleting (the home page opening in place of the deleted chat), search by what an agent wrote, a title given at the start; dragging a conversation into a group and out, and a branched family moving as one; every CLI's health dot and the count on one line, the chevron's tip at the report icon's edge; the star left of the report icon: its dialog, its picture's press (played three times; not with reduced motion) and its link to the repository in a new tab.
- `e2e/specs/models.spec.ts` — a member's model and effort reaching each CLI (Claude Code's flags, Codex's thread, Grok's session options, the others as their adapters pass them; an open agent's model from the fake endpoint), and Settings' default model and effort for a member left on Default.
- `e2e/specs/tools.spec.ts` — the tool library's page, with a member of each CLI, in two teams: an MCP server (its secret masked and off the command line), a skill picked from a folder and read by each CLI, memory for all and for one CLI, each turned off for a CLI or in a member's Tools; an MCP server given a new value and removed, and a skill and a memory note removed. Also what a member's Tools say of its own CLI's setup: nothing while the library loads, then with the library empty, with nothing for its CLI, and over its items.
- `e2e/specs/share.spec.ts` — copying a reply's Markdown and the conversation (Chromium); sharing as a secret gist through the fake `gh`, listed in Settings and removed; `gh` signed out (signed in from the dialog with GitHub's code, then the link made once, also on a slow network; cancelled and started again; refused by GitHub; signed in from Settings' removal), and missing (not on Windows).
- `e2e/specs/accounts.spec.ts` — for each CLI with a sign-in: an added account's own home (Cursor's, Antigravity's and Muse Code's signed in with an API key; Copilot's sign-in asked in a home of the question's own), signing in and out per account (Claude Code's sign-in flow; Codex, Grok and Copilot signed out on their own), an account's API key with the credential store off (Claude Code, Grok, Cursor, Antigravity, Copilot and Muse Code: masked, given to its CLI only; Codex: through `codex login --with-api-key` in its folder), the server's own key for the default account only, removing an account (signed out, its folder deleted, its member and the lead agent on the default account), and no removal while a conversation runs; an account added while an older status answer is still on its way keeping the newer answer's state (that answer held back with `page.route`), and a CLI signed out outside Harness Relay shown so when Settings is opened again within the server's cache time (the app's cached answer held until Settings' own has landed).
- `e2e/specs/native.spec.ts` — what each CLI loads by itself: its own skill listed and left out of the next run (Claude Code, Codex), and Grok's trust in a folder (from the Grok card, and from a new chat's team card, trusted or declined).
- `e2e/specs/sessions.spec.ts` — the CLIs' own folder, in two teams: sessions kept there, a fresh session after it changes, folders refused, no change while a conversation runs; AgentsView (a stand-in server): a conversation and its members' sessions sent, a Cursor member's recorded session sent after the thread and linked under it, Copilot's and Muse Code's own sessions linked under it as `copilot:<id>` and `muse:<id>` with none recorded for them, an address elsewhere refused, a failed update shown in Settings.
- `e2e/specs/report.spec.ts` — a problem report: the mail's `mailto:` and Gmail links with the details, a draft kept, a report too long for the link, screenshots to upload first, and screenshots that cannot be uploaded (`gh` signed out), or uploaded again by themselves once gh is signed in from the dialog.
- `e2e/specs/appearance.spec.ts` — the guided tour (stepped through, skipped, replayed from Settings, on a phone a sheet at the bottom whose long step scrolls above its buttons, and on a wide screen Next in one place) and the theme (dark or light kept, following the system).
- `e2e/specs/templates.spec.ts` — the built-in templates and their members, the lead agent using one as it is, saving a designed team as a template; in a template's editor three columns wide, the add card on a row of its own and beside members; a new template's members on the installed CLIs only, also when opened before their statuses came, and a template's member on a CLI not installed moved to one that is, said so, and saveable.
- `e2e/specs/limits.spec.ts` — a server started with `RELAY_MAX_TEAM_AGENTS`, `RELAY_MAX_ACCOUNTS` and `RELAY_TURN_TIMEOUT_MIN`: `GET /api/limits`, the team editor adding no more members (and a larger template refused), Settings adding no more accounts, a built-in template (three members) saying why it cannot be used and the lead agent falling back to the general one cut to two, and a schedule's hint giving the timeout.
- `e2e/specs/schedule.spec.ts` — a chat's schedule saved and run once.
- `e2e/specs/settings.spec.ts` — the sidebar's health dots on one line (how many are ready, their names shown and kept after a reload); each CLI's version and sign-in (the open agents "Ready" on the fake endpoint), an executable path (a wrong one shows the CLI missing), the language kept after a reload, another CLI chosen as the lead agent; each CLI with a sign-in when it is not signed in (Settings, the sidebar, and its reply's error); Cursor and Antigravity not installed (`missingClis`); an open agent without a model backend not ready, and its turn's error (`openBackend: false`).
- `e2e/specs/windows.spec.ts` — on Windows only, the CLIs started through the launchers they come with there (Cursor's own `cursor-agent.cmd`, npm's shims for a Node script and for a program), set as their executable path in Settings: each found with its version, and answering.
- `e2e/specs/signin.spec.ts` — the sign-in page, a wrong code, signing in with a code, a one-time sign-in link (a used-up one shows its error), a link opened in a tab that shows the sign-in page already, a link's error gone after signing in with a code and being signed out, a link opened in a tab signed in already that keeps it signed in while requests sent with the old key are still on their way (their answers, "signed out", reach the page together with the sign-in's; never a sign-out), a tab showing the sign-in page following a sign-in in another tab of the browser, a link opened in another tab keeping every tab signed in while the first has requests on their way, a tab still on its first check following another tab's sign-in (its first answer out of date by then), a sign-in that gets no answer given up after 30 seconds with a message (on the page's clock), signing out in Settings; in a development build of the UI (React's StrictMode), a link opening the app on the first load (asking once whether the browser is signed in), a wrong one showing its error, the same link in a tab signed in already, and a link opened in another tab.
- `e2e/specs/voice.spec.ts` — voice input's install in Settings (its status from GET /api/voice with an install put in, since a real one downloads): its steps in line, their state for screen readers (the current one `aria-current`), the download's percent and its bar's fill; a failed install's stopped step.
- `e2e/specs/i18n.spec.ts` — the UI (the home page's tagline too) and the lead agent's card in Traditional Chinese with the prompts in English; the sign-in page and the server's own messages in Chinese.
- `e2e/specs/mobile.spec.ts` — a phone's screen: the sidebar from the menu, sending by touch, the header's actions under ⋯, no sideways scrolling; at 320px and 280px, a template's editor, the team settings, a new schedule of each kind (in both languages, English also at 400px and 430px and Chinese at 250px, with the rows the four kinds of schedule and the week's days take) and a chat's Schedule without sideways scrolling, and the add card listing every CLI in one column; the home page's tagline smaller with each line unwrapped, and the logo closing the sidebar.

The fakes check Harness Relay's side of each protocol, not the CLIs' own: a CLI that changes what it writes is caught by the adapter tests' recorded messages and by the [live tests](#live-tests), which run the real CLIs. Nor do they run the CLIs' own sandboxes: the fakes of Claude Code, Codex, Grok, Copilot and Muse Code work out what theirs would allow, while the open agents' fakes run in Harness Relay's own sandbox for real; that the real CLIs below full access write nothing outside the chat's folders is a live test (`e2e/live/sandbox.spec.ts`).

## Live tests

```bash
E2E_LIVE_OLLAMA=http://127.0.0.1:11434/v1 npm run test:e2e:live
E2E_LIVE_OLLAMA=http://127.0.0.1:11434/v1 E2E_LIVE_OLLAMA_TYPED=gemma4:26b E2E_LIVE_CLOUD=1 npm run test:e2e:live
E2E_LIVE_VLLM=http://127.0.0.1:8000/v1 E2E_LIVE_VLLM_MODEL=<its served name> E2E_LIVE_VLLM_KEY=<its key> npm run test:e2e:live
E2E_LIVE_OLLAMA=… E2E_LIVE_CLIS=opencode,claude npx playwright test -c playwright.live.config.ts -g "Team settings"
E2E_LIVE_CLOUD=1 E2E_LIVE_CLIS=antigravity E2E_LIVE_MODEL_ANTIGRAVITY=gemini-3.8-flash npm run test:e2e:live
npx playwright show-report playwright-report-live
```

The same server and UI with the real CLIs, signed in as you are, on real models: an Ollama or vLLM server of your own (any OpenAI-compatible endpoint), and with `E2E_LIVE_CLOUD=1` the CLIs' own cloud models, which your sign-ins pay for. They never run on CI: each test is skipped unless the environment names what it needs. Run them on each system you can when you change an adapter, the sandboxes or the model backends; they find what the fakes cannot (a CLI that only runs with full access on one system, or writes somewhere without asking). One test at a time ([`playwright.live.config.ts`](../playwright.live.config.ts): one worker, up to 20 minutes a test, the report in `playwright-report-live/`), since a turn on a real model is slow and the CLIs share your own folders.

- **What they run with** (`startRelay({ live: true })`, through `test.use({ live: true })`): each test still has a server of its own, with its own data folder, working directories and `RELAY_KEYRING=off` (an endpoint's key stays in that test's database, encrypted), but `HOME` and each CLI's own folder are yours, so the CLIs run with your sign-ins and their sessions land in your folders; no fake comes first on the `PATH`, and no CLI is set up to be missing. A CLI that is not on the `PATH` is named by `E2E_LIVE_BIN_<PROVIDER>` (`E2E_LIVE_BIN_OPENHANDS=~/.local/bin/openhands`). On macOS and Linux the server's folder is in `/tmp`: Harness Relay's own sandbox needs a short path for its sockets, and macOS's temporary folder is too deep. Since the CLIs run as you, an approval is given only for what the tests ask (`liveAllows()`: a command that writes `hi` into a file, a write in the chat's own folders); anything else is refused. What any run of Harness Relay leaves in the CLIs' own folders stays there: the endpoints' models block in Grok's `config.toml`, the CLIs' sessions, and the trust a CLI records for a project folder (here temporary ones).
- **The environment**: `E2E_LIVE_OLLAMA` (a base URL ending in `/v1`) and `E2E_LIVE_OLLAMA_MODEL` (default `gemma4:e4b`), `E2E_LIVE_OLLAMA_TYPED` (another model that server has, for a model typed for the endpoint), `E2E_LIVE_VLLM`, `E2E_LIVE_VLLM_MODEL` and `E2E_LIVE_VLLM_KEY`, `E2E_LIVE_CLIS` (the CLIs to try, comma-separated; default all twelve), `E2E_LIVE_CLOUD=1` (also the CLIs' own cloud models: Claude Code's, Codex's, Grok's, Cursor's, Antigravity's, Copilot's and Muse Code's, `CLOUD_CLIS` in `live.ts`) and `E2E_LIVE_MODEL_<CLI>` (a model of that CLI's own list, picked in Team settings for its cloud tests, such as `E2E_LIVE_MODEL_ANTIGRAVITY=gemini-3.8-flash`; unset, its default model). Each test sets the endpoints up in its server's Settings, and lets OpenHands use a terminal in the sandbox, which it needs below full access. Antigravity has no custom endpoints, so it runs only with `E2E_LIVE_CLOUD=1`, signed in as agy keeps it (with Google, in your `~/.gemini` and the system keyring) or with `GEMINI_API_KEY` in your environment, which live runs keep. Copilot runs on an endpoint through its BYOK variables, with no GitHub sign-in, and on its own models with `E2E_LIVE_CLOUD=1`, signed in as it keeps it (with GitHub, in your `~/.copilot` and the system's credential store) or with `COPILOT_GITHUB_TOKEN`, `GH_TOKEN` or `GITHUB_TOKEN` in your environment. Neither has Muse Code: it runs only with `E2E_LIVE_CLOUD=1`, signed in as muse keeps it (with a Meta account, in your `~/.config/muse` and, on macOS, the login keychain) or with `META_API_KEY` in your environment. Muse Code was checked only with a fake Meta API key against a stand-in for the Meta API: its live tests have not been run on real models yet.
- **What they check** ([`e2e/live/`](../e2e/live/), the helpers in `live.ts`):
  - `models.spec.ts` — each CLI that runs on an endpoint (Claude Code, Codex, Grok, Copilot and the five open agents) on each endpoint given: its model picked in Team settings from the model menu, which lists it, saved, and a question answered (17 + 25), with Claude Code's, Codex's, Grok's and Copilot's session recorded on that endpoint (`agent_sessions.backend`, read from the test's database: not their own service); the same four with the endpoint's model as their default model, picked on their card in Settings under **Your own endpoints**, for a member without a model of its own; with `E2E_LIVE_CLOUD=1`, each of `CLOUD_CLIS` on its own default model, and on `E2E_LIVE_MODEL_<CLI>` picked in Team settings when it is set (Claude Code's, Codex's, Grok's and Copilot's session recorded on their own service); a member on a model of a deleted endpoint failing its turn with the reason (the first of Claude Code, Codex, Grok and Copilot in `E2E_LIVE_CLIS`: the endpoint was deleted and the turn did not run; the first open agent: there is no such endpoint); Grok on a model typed for the endpoint that its list leaves out (`E2E_LIVE_OLLAMA_TYPED`); on Windows, an open agent below full access not running, and saying why.
  - `teams.spec.ts` — one team of up to three CLIs on their own models (Claude Code first) and two on the endpoints, each answering a question of its own (`E2E_LIVE_CLOUD=1`); each CLI writing a file in each of two working directories through its shell, in edit mode, on the first endpoint's model, and with `E2E_LIVE_CLOUD=1` each of `CLOUD_CLIS` on its own model too (Claude Code, Codex, Grok, Copilot and Muse Code in their own sandboxes, Cursor and Antigravity asking before each command; the open agents on Windows and OpenCode on Linux with full access, the only access they run with there); Cursor writing in the second one with its own file tool, its card saying the file is in another of the chat's working directories (in folders under your home, removed afterwards, since Cursor writes in a temporary folder without asking); Antigravity, with `E2E_LIVE_CLOUD=1`, ending its turn at a refused command (`echo no >`, which `liveAllows()` refuses: no file, the refused call says that Antigravity ends its turn after a refusal, and nothing follows it), and asking about a project's own MCP server (`.agents/mcp_config.json`) before agy starts: refused, the turn does not start and the server never runs; Copilot, on the first endpoint's model or with `E2E_LIVE_CLOUD=1` on its own, ending its turn at a refused command (`echo no >` in a read-only chat, which it asks about and `liveAllows()` refuses: no file, the refused call says that Copilot ends its turn after a refusal, and nothing follows it), and asking about a project's own hook (a `sessionStart` hook in `.github/hooks/`) before it starts: refused, the turn does not start and the hook never runs.
  - `sandbox.spec.ts` — what the fakes cannot show: each CLI that runs below full access here, asked in edit mode to write a file outside the chat's folders (`echo hi >` into a folder under your home, not a temporary one), with the chat refusing on its own (`approvals: 'deny'`), writes nothing there: Claude Code, Codex, Grok, Copilot and Muse Code kept in by their own sandboxes, the open agents by Harness Relay's, and Cursor and Antigravity, which have none, by asking. On an endpoint's model, or the CLI's own with `E2E_LIVE_CLOUD=1`; skipped when the model did not even try.
- A small model may not do what it is asked (call its shell tool, answer with the number alone), so a failed check says what the turn did: its error, and the tools it called. `E2E_LIVE_OLLAMA_MODEL` picks a stronger one.

## Continuous integration

[`.github/workflows/ci.yml`](../.github/workflows/ci.yml) runs on every pull request and every push to `main`: `npm ci`, `npm run typecheck`, `npm test` and `npm run build`, on Ubuntu with Node 22 (the oldest supported) and Node 24, and on Windows (`windows-latest`) with Node 24, where the tests' temporary folder is on `D:` with the checkout (`runner.temp`) rather than on `C:`. On Ubuntu it also installs the desktop app's tools (without the Electron binary), typechecks the desktop app, builds it (`desktop/scripts/build.ts`) and runs its bundled server as the app does (`desktop/scripts/smoke.ts`). A second job (`desktop-package`) packages the desktop app without an installer, for Linux (`electron-builder --linux dir`, for x64 and on GitHub's Arm runner for Arm64 as a release does) and for Windows x64 (`--win dir`; Windows on Arm is checked by the Release workflow), and checks it as a release is checked (`desktop/scripts/check-package.ts`: on Linux on a virtual display, with Electron's own libraries installed where the runner lacks them), so on every pull request the packaged app has a team of the fake CLIs answer a question on Linux and Windows. That job builds the app with a made-up `RELAY_UPDATES_TOKEN`, put together in the shell, which the check must then find in the app, encoded only. Releases have a workflow of their own: see [Releasing](#releasing). A new push to a pull request cancels its previous run. Run the same commands locally before pushing.

[`.github/workflows/e2e.yml`](../.github/workflows/e2e.yml) runs the [end-to-end tests](#end-to-end-tests) (`npm run test:e2e`, Chromium) on Ubuntu, macOS and Windows with Node 24, and on Ubuntu with Node 22 too, for every pull request and every push to `main` (each in two jobs, half the tests each, `--shard=1/2` and `2/2`; up to 45 minutes a job); run it by hand (**Actions → End-to-end tests → Run workflow**) to choose other browsers. Before the tests it sets up Harness Relay's own sandbox, which the open agents' fakes run in below full access: on Ubuntu it installs bubblewrap, socat and ripgrep where the runner lacks them and, where Ubuntu restricts unprivileged user namespaces, an AppArmor profile that lets `/usr/bin/bwrap` make them; on Ubuntu it then runs the sandbox's own checks (`npx tsx scripts/sandbox-check.ts`; not on macOS, whose runners' curl, on macOS 26.7, cannot verify a certificate inside the sandbox, so the checks that reach example.com fail there), and on Ubuntu and macOS the tests with `E2E_REQUIRE_RELAY_SANDBOX=1`, so a test that needs the sandbox fails there rather than skips. It runs them with `E2E_SYSTEM_CLIS=1`: each CLI's own tests for all twelve on Ubuntu, and on macOS and Windows for the CLIs Harness Relay runs differently there ([e2e/README.md, Platforms and browsers](../e2e/README.md#platforms-and-browsers)); run it by hand with **every_cli** for all twelve everywhere. Each job uploads the HTML report, with the trace, screenshot, video and logs of each failed test (`e2e-report-<os>-node<version>-part<1 or 2>`).

To block merging until the checks pass, add a branch protection rule (or ruleset) for `main` in the repository settings and require the `Typecheck, test, build` checks (and `End-to-end` ones, if you want them too).

### Website

The landing page at https://agentic-system-lab.github.io/harness-relay/ is plain HTML, CSS and JavaScript in [`site/`](../site/): `index.html` (English), `zh-TW/index.html` (繁體中文) and shared `assets/`. There is no build step. [`.github/workflows/pages.yml`](../.github/workflows/pages.yml) runs on pushes to `main` that touch `site/`, `docs/media/` or the workflow itself: it copies `site/` and the demo videos from `docs/media/` into one folder, leaves out Markdown files, and deploys it to GitHub Pages (repository settings → Pages → Source: GitHub Actions). Preview it with `npm run site:preview` ([`scripts/preview-site.ts`](../scripts/preview-site.ts)): it serves `site/` on http://127.0.0.1:4180 (`SITE_PORT` changes the port) with the videos read from `docs/media/`, the way Pages will. Read [`site/DESIGN-GUIDELINES.md`](../site/DESIGN-GUIDELINES.md) before changing the page; it also says what to update when the demo is re-recorded.

### Recording the demo

The demo in the READMEs and on the landing page (`docs/media/demo-*.mp4` and `.webp`) is a real run, recorded by `npm run demo` ([`scripts/demo/`](../scripts/demo/)). Record it again when the UI it passes through changes. It needs the three CLIs installed and signed in, FFmpeg and libwebp (`brew install ffmpeg webp` on macOS), and the Chromium that `playwright-core` drives (`npx playwright-core install chromium-headless-shell` once, or `DEMO_CHROME` set to a Chrome or Chromium binary).

```bash
npm run demo                          # record both languages, cut them and publish them
npm run demo -- record --dry          # check the UI flow up to the open chat, no agents (under a minute)
npm run demo -- record --lang en      # one step, one language
npm run demo -- cut publish           # cut and publish the last recordings again
```

1. **record** builds the UI and starts Harness Relay on its own port (8791, and 8792 for the second language) with an empty data folder in `data/demo/<lang>/`, so your own conversations and settings are left alone; this repository is the chat's working directory, read-only. A headless browser then does what a person would, with a drawn cursor: a new group, the Three-way debate template, a new chat and a question that names all three CLIs, the lead agent's team (confirmed), the answers (a request beyond read-only is shown and declined, a question the agents ask is skipped), a follow-up question with a team of its own, Files at relay mode's hand-off in `orchestrator.ts`, and a search. Both languages record at once, about half an hour with the real CLIs. Each step leaves a mark in `marks.json`.
2. **cut** speeds the agents' work up so the video lasts about two minutes (`--length`), and writes `video.mp4` (1600×1000, 24 fps), `animation.webp` (880×550, 8 fps) and `timeline.json`. If the recorder waited for nothing, add a `skip-start` / `skip-end` pair to `marks.json` and cut again.
3. **publish** copies the video and the animation into `docs/media/`, re-cuts the posters, the screenshots and the social cards from the frame where the first team's card shows (the `poster` mark), re-times the chapter rails in `site/index.html` and `site/zh-TW/index.html` and the frame times in `site/DESIGN-GUIDELINES.md`, and writes `data/demo/<lang>/review.md`: the chapters as written, the teams, any approval, the search term and the replies.

Then read `review.md` and watch the videos. The chapter sentences are in `scripts/demo/chapters.ts`; the teams, names, rounds, the group and the search term are filled in from the recording, but when the agents did something the sentences don't describe, change them there and run `npm run demo -- publish`. Check the READMEs' captions and the rail notes too, then commit the media, `site/` and the scripts together.

## Releasing

A release is the desktop app's installers for macOS (a `.dmg` for Apple Silicon), Windows (x64 and Arm64 `.exe`) and Linux (x64 and Arm64 `.AppImage`), published together as one GitHub release by the Release workflow ([`.github/workflows/release.yml`](../.github/workflows/release.yml)). It is run by hand; nothing else makes releases or tags.

**Versions** are the day of the release in Taiwan (`RELEASE_TIME_ZONE`, Asia/Taipei), and the git tag is the version itself:

| Version | Is |
| --- | --- |
| `YYYY-MM-DD-rc` | a release candidate of the day's release, published as a pre-release |
| `YYYY-MM-DD-rc.1`, `.2`, … | that day's patches of it |
| `YYYY-MM-DD` | a release |
| `YYYY-MM-DD.1`, `.2`, … | that day's patches of it |
| `YYYY-MM-DD.1-rc`, `YYYY-MM-DD.2-rc`, … | a release candidate of that patch, published as a pre-release |
| `YYYY-MM-DD.1-rc.1`, `.2`, … | that day's patches of it |

A fix on a later day is that day's release, not a patch. A release candidate is optional, and is always one of the day's next release: the release itself until it is out, then its next patch (`npm run release:next` prints both). One of a release that is out is refused: it would sort before that release (`2026.1110.0-rc.2` < `2026.1110.0`), and the download page, which offers the newest release by publish time, would show it instead (the app, which goes by version, would never offer it). A release candidate of a patch sorts between the release before it and the patch (`2026.1110.0` < `2026.1110.1-rc` < `2026.1110.1-rc.1` < `2026.1110.1`), so both find the same newest one.

**To release:**

1. On `main`, see today's next versions: `npm run release:next` (it reads the tags on GitHub).
2. In GitHub, open **Actions → Release → Run workflow**, keep **Use workflow from: main**, enter the version, and run it.

`npm run release:check -- <version>` checks a version locally the same way (against your local tags; `npm run release:check -- <version> --remote` for GitHub's: without the `--`, npm keeps `--remote` for itself), and says which version its notes would start from. `npm run release:notes -- <version> --remote` prints the list of changes its notes have by these rules (`--target <commit>` for a release not made yet: a SHA, a tag or a branch on GitHub, `main` rather than `origin/main`), read from GitHub through your `gh`.

**What the notes list.** Every commit that came into `main` since an earlier version, up to the commit the release is made on, whether it was pushed to `main` or merged from a pull request (`scripts/release.ts notes`, `releaseChanges()`): the commits GitHub's comparison of the two lists (`git log <earlier>..<commit>`, the commits a merge brought in included), oldest first, each by its subject. A commit that merged a pull request (squashed, rebased or fast-forwarded, as GitHub records the pull request's merge commit) links to the pull request, without the ` (#N)` a squash ends its subject with, and is by whoever opened it; any other commit links to itself and is by its author. A merge commit in git's words ("Merge pull request …", "Merge branch …", "Merge main into …") says nothing of its own: one that merged a pull request is listed by the pull request's title, any other is left out, since the commits it brought in are listed. A subject stays one line of text: a leading `#`, `>`, `-`, `+`, `*` or `1.` is escaped, and an `@name` in it (outside code and links, with what is attached to it, or in the git name of an author without an account) is put in code, so that the notes notify nobody it merely names. Then the authors with no commit before the version the notes start from, by their account or by an address of theirs in the list (GitHub finds a bot's commits by its address only), as New Contributors, each with their first pull request in the list (else their first change), and the link to the comparison (Full Changelog), as GitHub's own notes have them (a bot as `@name`, without `[bot]`). GitHub asks are made eight at a time, one for each commit's pull request. A list longer than 100,000 characters (GitHub takes 125,000 for a release's notes, the install notes included) ends with how many more changes there are, linked to the history up to the tag (GitHub's comparison page shows 250 commits at most). GitHub's own notes (`--generate-notes`) list pull requests only, and left out what was pushed to `main` straight.

**Where the notes start** (`previousVersion()` in `scripts/release.ts`):

- a release starts at the release before it, so that its notes hold every change since then, its release candidates' included (`2026-11-12` from `2026-11-11.1`, not from `2026-11-12-rc.1`);
- a release candidate starts at the version before it, release or release candidate (`2026-11-12-rc` from `2026-11-11.1`, `2026-11-12-rc.1` from `2026-11-12-rc`).

"Before" goes by version (semver), not by when the tags were made. When there is no such version, the notes list every commit up to the release, from the repository's first.

**What the workflow checks** (`scripts/release.ts`, tested in `test/release.test.ts`), before it builds anything:

- the version follows the rules, with a precise reason when it does not: a `v` in front, `-RC` in capitals, `-rc1` without the dot, `.0`, `.01`, one-digit months or days, a day that is not in the calendar (`2026-02-30`), spaces, or a character that only looks like one (a full-width digit, an en dash, a non-breaking hyphen);
- its date is today in Taiwan, not yesterday or tomorrow (also around midnight UTC, when the two days differ);
- it is the next one: not released already, no patch before the release it patches, no skipped number, a release candidate only of the day's next release (none of a release that is out or of a later patch than the next). The error says which versions today takes;
- the run is on `main`;
- the app's update source (the `tokens` job and `desktop/src/updateSource.ts`, tested in `test/desktop-update.test.ts`): first of all, in a job of its own (`tokens`), that the write token is set, and that the read token is not the write token (trimmed, as the build reads them), with a warning when GitHub says both belong to one account, or does not say whose they are (a token expired or revoked, say); then, before anything is installed, that the repository is `owner/name` and not this one, that the read token is a fine-grained one (`updateSourceFrom()`), and that the repository's releases can be read as an app built with them would read them (`repositoryReadable()`: `releases?per_page=1`, with the read token, else with the job's own token in its place; the repository itself answers a token without Contents: Read too). A read token without access to it or to its contents, expired, revoked or waiting for an organization's approval, a private repository without one, or a repository moved (GitHub's 301) stops the run before anything is built; GitHub failing, its limit on requests or no answer only warns, as the copies' check asks again after the builds.

Then it runs the typecheck and the tests, builds the five installers in parallel, the Arm64 ones on GitHub's Arm runners (each runs `desktop/scripts/smoke.ts` on its bundled server first, and `desktop/scripts/check-package.ts` on the packaged app after: fuses, resources, no token in the server's files nor as text in the app's, the repository and the read token it was built with, encoded, and the app's own `--smoke-test`, a question to the fake CLIs included, under `xvfb-run` on Linux), checks the version again against the tags as they are now (a run that started before midnight keeps its day), checks that there is exactly one installer for each end of name in `INSTALLERS` (`site/assets/downloads.js`, which the website and the app's updates look for) and nothing else, copies each one for the app's updates (`updateCopyName()`: `…-mac-arm64-update.dmg` and so on, which the app downloads instead, so that the download counts tell updates from new installs; the website never offers them. Apps released before 2026-11-12-rc still download the installers, and 2026-11-12-rc looks for the copies in the release itself, so their updates count as installs until their users have updated once), checks that every installer was built with the repository and read token the run has now (each build's fingerprint, `sameUpdateSource()`: a run of the failed jobs alone builds nothing again), publishes the copies under the same tag in the repository [`Agentic-System-Lab/harness-relay-updates`](https://github.com/Agentic-System-Lab/harness-relay-updates) (`UPDATES_REPO`, or the one the variable of that name names, below) first, so that no app is offered the release before its copies are there, then checks that each copy is listed whole where the app will ask for it (`copiesReadable()`: asked with the read token, else the job's own, and downloading nothing, so that no download is counted; a private repository without a read token, or a token that cannot read it, stops the run here, before the release is published; a failure of GitHub's is asked again, three times in all, a 404 once more (a release just made may not be found at once), and its limit on requests, its secondary one too, waited out when all the waits together come within five minutes, else left as a warning; a repository renamed or moved (GitHub's 301, not followed) says to set the variable to its new name; a read token that expires within 90 days is warned about), uploads a release's installers to the download mirror when the website has one and checks that the mirror serves each of them whole (below; a release candidate never goes there), and then publishes the release on the commit it built, with the installers named `Harness-Relay-<version>-<os>-<arch>.<ext>`, notes listing every commit into `main` since the version they start from (above; by the tags of that last check, and listed before anything is published, so that a failure there publishes nothing), and the install notes (the systems each installer needs, the first-run warning on each system, `--no-sandbox` on Ubuntu 24.04, who is offered it: an installed Harness Relay, in its menu and its chat, a release candidate only to release candidates and builds of your own). Only one release runs at a time, and only the publishing job may write to the repository; it writes to the copies' repository with a token of its own. The write token (`UPDATES_TOKEN`) goes to four steps only: the three of the first job, `tokens` (that it is set, the comparison with the read token, and the question of whose they are, each token given to `curl` on its standard input, never among its arguments), which checks nothing out and installs nothing, and the step that publishes the copies, in a job that installs no npm package either. A runner keeps the secrets a job names in its memory for the whole job, so no job that installs packages names it. The read token (`UPDATES_READ_TOKEN`), when there is one, goes to the last two of those, the check of the update source, the app's build and the packaged app's checks, and the release job's two update checks. The comparison comes before anything is built and its artifacts uploaded, which anyone who may read this repository may download. The version reaches the scripts through the environment, never in a command line.

**The copies' token.** The workflow's own token may write to this repository only, so the copies go up with the `UPDATES_TOKEN` secret: a fine-grained personal access token whose resource owner is the owner of the copies' repository (Agentic-System-Lab), for that repository only (`harness-relay-updates`, or the one the `UPDATES_REPO` variable names), with **Contents: Read and write**. Set it with `gh secret set UPDATES_TOKEN --repo Agentic-System-Lab/harness-relay` (it asks for the value). Without it, the copies' step stops with an error naming the secret; once it expires, `gh` fails there with GitHub's 401. Either way nothing is published: make a new token and set it again.

**Where the copies are.** An app downloads its updates from the repository the copies go to, built into it (`RELAY_UPDATES_REPO`, [above](#the-desktop-app)): `Agentic-System-Lab/harness-relay-updates`, or the one the repository variable `UPDATES_REPO` names (`gh variable set UPDATES_REPO --body <owner/name> --repo Agentic-System-Lab/harness-relay`; never this repository, which the build refuses); the check of the update source, the build and the packaged app's checks, the fingerprints' check, the copies' step and the check after it all follow it, read the same way (`updateSourceFrom()`). The versions and their checksums always come from this repository's releases, so wherever the copies cannot be had the app downloads the installer itself, and its update only counts as a new install. That is how the apps built before a change update once it is made: those built for the old repository after the variable names another, and those built without a token once the copies' repository goes private.

To move the copies to another repository:

1. Make the repository, with nothing in it but the copies to come.
2. Let `UPDATES_TOKEN` write to it (or set a new one that may).
3. Set the `UPDATES_REPO` variable to it.
4. If it is private, set `UPDATES_READ_TOKEN` to a token for it (below); if it is public, delete that secret (a token left there would still go into every app).
5. Run the workflow for the next release.

For a private repository, set the `UPDATES_READ_TOKEN` secret as well (`gh secret set UPDATES_READ_TOKEN --repo Agentic-System-Lab/harness-relay`, which asks for the value): a fine-grained personal access token (the build refuses any other kind) for that repository only, with **Contents: Read-only** and nothing else beside the Metadata: Read-only that GitHub adds to every token. Never the write token: the workflow refuses `UPDATES_TOKEN` there before it builds anything, and with it anyone could replace the copies (an app then still updates, from the installer, after the copy fails its checksum). If one is set there by mistake all the same, revoke it. A different token that may also write is not caught: check its permissions when you make it.

- **Anyone can read it.** It goes into every installer, where anyone can read it, so it must allow nothing else. **Contents: Read-only** shows the whole repository: keep nothing in it but the copies. The build encodes it (`encodeUpdateSource()`), so that it never stands in the app as a token's text: GitHub revokes a token it finds pushed to a public repository or gist, and anyone could push an unpacked app. That keeps scanners off it, not people.
- **Owner and approval.** Its resource owner must be the repository's owner. For an organization's repository, the organization may have to approve it first (until then it reads nothing), and its policy may cap how long a token lives. Make it with an account of its own, not the write token's: the requests below are counted per account, and anyone with the app can use up that account's with it, which would then hold up publishing the copies with the write token too. The workflow warns when GitHub says both belong to one account, and when it does not say whose they are.
- **Requests.** An app sends it only when it updates, never when it checks for one: one question for the release (`releases/tags/<tag>`) and one for the copy's address, and one more each time a signed address expires during the download. An app built with it does so for a public repository too, the copy then also coming through GitHub's API. These come from the 5,000 requests an hour of the token's account, shared by every installed app.
- **When it stops working.** Once it expires or is revoked, the apps built with it count their updates as new installs until they update to a release built with a new one; they still update. Installed apps keep the token they were built with, so to replace it: set the new secret, publish a release with it while the old one still works, and revoke the old one later (the apps not updated by then count their next update as a new install). If it is misused, or turns out to allow more than reading the copies, revoke it at once: apps keep updating, from the installers. Give it no expiry where the organization allows; the copies' check warns when it expires within 90 days (`expiryWarning()`, from the expiry GitHub gives with an answer).
- **The checks.** Before anything is built, the workflow asks GitHub for the repository's releases with the token, and before it publishes the release, it checks that each copy is listed whole where the app will ask for it, with the token again. Without the secret, a private repository stops the run at the first of these. Once you set or change the secret (or the variable), run the whole workflow again (**Re-run all jobs**, or a new run of the same version): a run of the failed jobs alone keeps the installers built before, which the fingerprint check (`sameUpdateSource()`) refuses before the copies are published. When nothing needs changing (GitHub failing, a copy not uploaded whole, an organization's approval given since), **Re-run failed jobs** is enough; each error of the copies' check says which, the token's saying both, since a token refused may only have waited for that approval. When GitHub's limit on requests keeps the copies' check from finishing, the release is published with a warning: the apps then update from the installers until they can have the copies. A run that stops at the copies' check has already published them under its tag: the next run replaces them, and if that version is dropped, delete them from the copies' repository by hand. To check a token yourself, without it in your shell's history: `read -rs GH_TOKEN && export GH_TOKEN && gh api repos/<owner>/<name>/releases/tags/<tag> --jq '.assets[].name'`.
- **Local builds.** Don't keep `RELAY_UPDATES_TOKEN` or `RELAY_UPDATES_REPO` in your shell's profile: every `npm run desktop` and `npm run desktop:dist` would build them in, and the app would download its updates as they say.

**Download counts.** GitHub counts each installer's downloads (not installs, and repeated downloads count again): new installs on this repository's releases (from the website, its download mirror included, and from the releases page), updates of an installed app on the copies' repository (`harness-relay-updates`, or the one `UPDATES_REPO` names; an update downloaded from the mirror too, which the app counts there itself). GitHub counts requests, not finished downloads: a request for the installer's address on github.com counts, a `HEAD` too and one whose redirect is not followed, and requests to GitHub's CDN at the address it redirects to count as well (not each one: three in a row at one address were seen to count once). A browser's download from GitHub makes both, and was seen to count as 2, as does the mirror's request for it (below). A count shows up a minute or two later for github.com's request, up to a quarter of an hour later for the CDN's:

```bash
gh api repos/Agentic-System-Lab/harness-relay/releases --paginate --jq '.[] | .tag_name as $t | .assets[] | "\($t)\t\(.name)\t\(.download_count)"'
gh api repos/<owner>/<name>/releases --paginate --jq '.[] | .tag_name as $t | .assets[] | "\($t)\t\(.name)\t\(.download_count)"'   # the copies' repository
```

**The download mirror.** GitHub's downloads are slow in parts of the world: from Taiwan (a HiNet connection), its CDN sends an installer at some 0.25 MB/s on one connection (8 to 9 minutes for one of 130 MB) and 1.5 MB/s on six, where the mirror gave 7 to 10 MB/s on one and 5 to 10 MB/s on six. So the website's download buttons, and the app's updates, can use a mirror instead: a Cloudflare Worker ([`mirror/worker.ts`](../mirror/worker.ts), `harness-relay-downloads`) in front of an R2 bucket (`harness-relay-mirror`), at `/<tag>/<file>` as on GitHub, whose address is `MIRROR` in `site/assets/downloads.js` (empty: no mirror, the buttons point at GitHub).

- **What it serves.** A release's five installers only (`Harness-Relay-<tag>` and an end of `INSTALLERS`; nothing else in the bucket is served), as files to save, whole or in byte ranges (a resumed download, `If-Range`), with `GET` and `HEAD` only; and each under its copy's name for the app's updates (an end of `UPDATE_INSTALLERS`), the same file in the bucket. An installer the bucket does not have (an older release, removed from it), or one it fails to give (R2 out of order while the Worker runs), is redirected to GitHub.
- **Counted on GitHub.** For each download it serves itself, a `GET` of the whole file or of a part from its first byte (not a resumed or later part, a `HEAD` or a `304`), the Worker asks GitHub for the installer's first byte as a download there asks (`Range: bytes=0-0`, the redirect followed to GitHub's CDN, with the visitor's user agent), after the answer has started (`waitUntil`). So GitHub counts it as it counts a download from GitHub, and the counts above stay whole; GitHub not reached, it goes uncounted, and the visitor still gets the file.
- **For the app's updates.** An app built with `MIRROR` set downloads its update from the mirror first, under the copy's name, and counts it on GitHub itself once it has the whole file, against the copy (the desktop app's **Updates**, [above](#the-desktop-app)); the mirror never counts those names, and answers 404 (not in the bucket) or 503 (the bucket failing) instead of a redirect, since a redirect to the installer would count as a new install: the app then downloads from GitHub as before. Apps built before `MIRROR` was set keep downloading from GitHub. The mirror holds releases only, so a release candidate updates from GitHub.
- **On the website.** The page finds the newest release on GitHub as before, then asks the mirror with a `HEAD` whether it has the visitor's installer (`mirrorHas()`; its redirect to GitHub not followed, since GitHub would count that); only when the mirror answers it has it do the links go to the mirror, else they stay on GitHub. The release kept in `localStorage` keeps GitHub's addresses.
- **In the Release workflow.** For a release (never a release candidate, which the website does not offer), when `MIRROR` is set: the installers uploaded to the bucket under the release's tag before the release is published (the runner's AWS CLI, at R2's S3 address), then each one's last byte asked of the mirror (`curl -r -1`, a redirect not followed: neither counts on GitHub), which must come from the bucket, before the release is published; after publishing, all but the newest two releases by version removed from the bucket (the one before may still be offered by a page that kept it, or be downloading), which keeps it within R2's free storage (10 GB; a release's five installers are some 650 MB). The upload and the pruning get the bucket's token; this job installs no npm package.

To set it up, once, with a Cloudflare account (R2 needs a payment method on file, though this stays within its free tier: storage above, no charge for what is downloaded, ten million reads a month):

1. In the Cloudflare dashboard, **R2 → Create bucket**, named `harness-relay-mirror` (`bucket_name` in `mirror/wrangler.toml`, `MIRROR_BUCKET` in the workflow), or `npx wrangler r2 bucket create harness-relay-mirror --location wnam`. Its location: **Western North America**. Cloudflare's free plan served the Worker to a HiNet connection in Taiwan from San Jose (`cf-ray` ending in `SJC`, where its own speed test is served from Taipei), and from there a bucket in Western North America read about as fast on one connection and two to three times as fast on six (the app's updates) as one in Asia-Pacific. A bucket's location is set the first time its name is created: deleting it and creating it again under the same name keeps the old location, so a new location needs a new name.
2. Deploy the Worker: `npx wrangler login`, then `npx wrangler deploy --config mirror/wrangler.toml`. It prints its address, `https://harness-relay-downloads.<subdomain>.workers.dev` (a custom domain can take its place, as a route). Deploy again after a change to `mirror/worker.ts` or to the names in `site/assets/downloads.js` it imports (`INSTALLERS`, `UPDATE_INSTALLERS`).
3. **R2 → Manage API tokens → Create API token**: **Object Read & Write**, for that bucket only. Set its access key ID and secret access key, and the account's S3 address it shows (`https://<account id>.r2.cloudflarestorage.com`): `gh secret set MIRROR_ACCESS_KEY_ID --repo Agentic-System-Lab/harness-relay`, `gh secret set MIRROR_SECRET_ACCESS_KEY --repo Agentic-System-Lab/harness-relay` (each asks for the value) and `gh variable set MIRROR_ENDPOINT --body https://<account id>.r2.cloudflarestorage.com --repo Agentic-System-Lab/harness-relay`.
4. Put the Worker's address in `MIRROR` in `site/assets/downloads.js` and commit it: the Pages workflow publishes the website with it, and the next release's run uploads its installers. The apps built from then on download their updates from the mirror (from the update to the release after theirs). Until a release is on the mirror, the website keeps GitHub's links (the mirror says it does not have them). With `MIRROR` set and the secrets or the variable missing, a release's run stops before it publishes anything.

To stop using it, empty `MIRROR`: the website goes back to GitHub's links and the workflow uploads nothing. Apps built with it still ask the mirror first: keep the Worker running (an empty bucket is enough: it answers 404, and they download from GitHub), or delete it, and they download from GitHub when it cannot be reached. To check it by hand without counting a download: `curl -sI <MIRROR>/<tag>/<file>` (200 from the bucket, 302 to GitHub without it).

## Debugging tips

- **Server logs** — the `[server]` lines in the `npm run dev` output; adapter failures include the CLI's stderr tail in the message's error.
- **SSE** — `curl -N -b 'relay_session=<cookie>' 'http://127.0.0.1:8789/api/conversations/<id>/events?key=<key>'` shows the raw event stream under `npm run dev` (8787 under `npm start`). Take the `relay_session` cookie and the key (`relay:key` in localStorage) from a signed-in browser's developer tools.
- **Language** — add `-H 'X-Relay-Lang: en'` to a `curl` call to see the English errors and templates.
- **Auto team** — the lead agent's run is a normal CLI session in the conversation's working directory; its reply and its cost are on the plan message (`messages.plan`, `cost_usd`).
- **Reproduce a CLI call** — the adapters' argument lists in `src/server/agents/*.ts` can be run by hand; add `--debug` (Claude, Grok) to see more.
- **Worktrees** — `git -C <working dir> worktree list` and `git branch --list 'relay/*'` show what parallel conversations created; `data/worktrees/<conversation>/` holds the checkouts.
- **Voice input** — `GET /api/voice` shows what is installed and the last install's log; the engine runs as `whisper-server` (see `ps`) and stops after 10 idle minutes. Downloads go to `data/voice/` (`models/`, and `engine/` on Linux and Windows), or to the folder `RELAY_VOICE_DIR` names; the log shows the address of each download.
- **Stale session** — with the server stopped, delete the agent's row in `agent_sessions` to force a fresh session with full history (open conversations are cached in memory, so edit the database only while the server is down).

## Conventions

- Strict TypeScript; shared types only in `src/shared/types.ts`.
- Never send secrets to the client (`SettingsView` returns masked keys only).
- New write endpoints automatically get the JSON content-type and Host/Origin checks from the `/api/*` middleware — keep it that way.
- Spawned processes go through `process.ts` so they are tracked and killed on shutdown.
